I mean, I know other people say they barely see anything, but in photos I just keep staring at that one uneven part. It’s hard to look past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or actions in an attempt to reduce your anxiety about the uneven part of your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Like before I send event photos or get on a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in my phone camera a few times, retake pictures, tilt my head different ways… sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, but it can take long enough that I get behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors, like retaking pictures or asking coworkers, in order to feel okay about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. I don’t really feel settled unless I check first. Like before sending photos or getting on a vendor meeting, I’ll look in my phone camera, retake pictures, tilt my head… sometimes ask someone if I look weird. It can take up enough time that I fall behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're checking your face in the phone camera or asking coworkers about how you look?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I start out thinking I’ll just check once, but then I keep looking in the camera or retaking it, trying different angles. And sometimes I still end up asking a coworker… It can kind of take over and make me late on planning things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s about my face. Since I’ve had to be in front of people more at work, I keep noticing this one part that looks uneven in photos. Other people say they can barely see it, but I can’t really stop looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Is it always this one part of your face that bothers you, or do you notice other areas as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly that one part of my face. It just looks uneven to me, especially in photos. I mean, that’s kind of why I came in. Since I’ve been in front of people more at work, I keep staring at that uneven part in photos, even though people tell me they barely see it. I just can’t seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the uneven part of your face been affecting your work or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly at work. I missed part of a planning deadline because I got stuck worrying about how I looked in the event materials. And now even things like vendor calls or meeting prep take me longer than they should.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry more about the uneven part of your face itself, or about what others might think of it?\n\nPatient: More about the part itself, I think. Like… I keep seeing it in photos, especially now that I’m more visible at work. That’s kind of why I came in. Since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, I just keep staring at that one uneven-looking part of my face in photos, even when everyone says they barely see it.\n\nDoctor: When you're worrying about the uneven part of your face, are you more concerned about the way it looks to you or the way you think it might look to others?\n\nPatient: I think… more the way it looks to me. I mean, I know other people say they barely see anything, but in photos I just keep staring at that one uneven part. It’s hard to look past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or actions in an attempt to reduce your anxiety about the uneven part of your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Like before I send event photos or get on a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in my phone camera a few times, retake pictures, tilt my head different ways… sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, but it can take long enough that I get behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors, like retaking pictures or asking coworkers, in order to feel okay about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. I don’t really feel settled unless I check first. Like before sending photos or getting on a vendor meeting, I’ll look in my phone camera, retake pictures, tilt my head… sometimes ask someone if I look weird. It can take up enough time that I fall behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're checking your face in the phone camera or asking coworkers about how you look?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I start out thinking I’ll just check once, but then I keep looking in the camera or retaking it, trying different angles. And sometimes I still end up asking a coworker… It can kind of take over and make me late on planning things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checking behaviors are something you can stop doing if you want to?\n\nPatient: I mean… I can stop eventually, but not really when I want to. If I’m about to send event pictures or get on a vendor call, I end up checking my face in the camera, retaking it, tilting my head… sometimes asking someone.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s about my face. Since I’ve had to be in front of people more at work, I keep noticing this one part that looks uneven in photos. Other people say they can barely see it, but I can’t really stop looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Is it always this one part of your face that bothers you, or do you notice other areas as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly that one part of my face. It just looks uneven to me, especially in photos. I know other people say they barely notice anything, but… I keep looking at it anyway.\n\nDoctor: How much distress does thinking about this uneven part of your face cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty upsetting. I mean, that’s kind of why I came in. Since I’ve been in front of people more at work, I keep staring at that uneven part in photos, even though people tell me they barely see it. I just can’t seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the uneven part of your face been affecting your work or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly at work. I missed part of a planning deadline because I got stuck worrying about how I looked in the event materials. And now even things like vendor calls or meeting prep take me longer than they should.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry more about the uneven part of your face itself, or about what others might think of it?\n\nPatient: More about the part itself, I think. Like… I keep seeing it in photos, especially now that I’m more visible at work. Other people say they barely notice anything, but I still can’t stop staring at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your life would be significantly better if you could just fix this uneven part of your face, regardless of what others think?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it would. That’s kind of why I came in. Since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, I just keep staring at that one uneven-looking part of my face in photos, even when everyone says they barely see it.\n\nDoctor: When you're worrying about the uneven part of your face, are you more concerned about the way it looks to you or the way you think it might look to others?\n\nPatient: I think… more the way it looks to me. I mean, I know other people say they barely see anything, but in photos I just keep staring at that one uneven part. It’s hard to look past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or actions in an attempt to reduce your anxiety about the uneven part of your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Like before I send event photos or get on a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in my phone camera a few times, retake pictures, tilt my head different ways… sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, but it can take long enough that I get behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors, like retaking pictures or asking coworkers, in order to feel okay about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. I don’t really feel settled unless I check first. Like before sending photos or getting on a vendor meeting, I’ll look in my phone camera, retake pictures, tilt my head… sometimes ask someone if I look weird. It can take up enough time that I fall behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're checking your face in the phone camera or asking coworkers about how you look?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I start out thinking I’ll just check once, but then I keep looking in the camera or retaking it, trying different angles. And sometimes I still end up asking a coworker… It can kind of take over and make me late on planning things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checking behaviors are something you can stop doing if you want to?\n\nPatient: I mean… I can stop eventually, but not really when I want to. If I’m about to send event pictures or get on a vendor call, I end up checking my face in the camera, retaking it, tilting my head… sometimes asking someone. It just kind of eats up time before I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face is something you do to try to make your anxiety about it go away?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s what I’m trying to do. Like if I check my face in the camera before sending event pictures or joining a vendor meeting, it feels like maybe I’ll calm down. But then I end up retaking photos or tilting my head, or asking a coworker if I look weird, and it can take long enough that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: Not really a number, no. It’s more like… before I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I keep checking my face in the phone camera, retaking it, tilting my head a certain way. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird. I stop when it feels “okay” enough, I guess, but it can take a while.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s about my face. Since I’ve had to be in front of people more at work, I keep noticing this one part that looks uneven in photos. Other people say they can barely see it, but I can’t really stop looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Is it always this one part of your face that bothers you, or do you notice other areas as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly that one part of my face. It just looks uneven to me, especially in photos. I know other people say they barely notice anything, but… I keep looking at it anyway.\n\nDoctor: How much distress does thinking about this uneven part of your face cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty upsetting. I mean, that’s kind of why I came in. Since I’ve been in front of people more at work, I keep staring at that uneven part in photos, even though people tell me they barely see it. I just can’t seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the uneven part of your face been affecting your work or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly at work. I missed part of a planning deadline because I got stuck worrying about how I looked in the event materials. And now even things like vendor calls or meeting prep take me longer than they should.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry more about the uneven part of your face itself, or about what others might think of it?\n\nPatient: More about the part itself, I think. Like… I keep seeing it in photos, especially now that I’m more visible at work. Other people say they barely notice anything, but I still can’t stop staring at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your life would be significantly better if you could just fix this uneven part of your face, regardless of what others think?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it would. That’s kind of why I came in. Since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, I just keep staring at that one uneven-looking part of my face in photos, even when everyone says they barely see it.\n\nDoctor: When you're worrying about the uneven part of your face, are you more concerned about the way it looks to you or the way you think it might look to others?\n\nPatient: I think… more the way it looks to me. I mean, I know other people say they barely see anything, but in photos I just keep staring at that one uneven part. It’s hard to look past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or actions in an attempt to reduce your anxiety about the uneven part of your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Like before I send event photos or get on a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in my phone camera a few times, retake pictures, tilt my head different ways… sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, but it can take long enough that I get behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors, like retaking pictures or asking coworkers, in order to feel okay about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. I don’t really feel settled unless I check first. Like before sending photos or getting on a vendor meeting, I’ll look in my phone camera, retake pictures, tilt my head… sometimes ask someone if I look weird. It can take up enough time that I fall behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're checking your face in the phone camera or asking coworkers about how you look?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I start out thinking I’ll just check once, but then I keep looking in the camera or retaking it, trying different angles. And sometimes I still end up asking a coworker… It can kind of take over and make me late on planning things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checking behaviors are something you can stop doing if you want to?\n\nPatient: I mean… I can stop eventually, but not really when I want to. If I’m about to send event pictures or get on a vendor call, I end up checking my face in the camera, retaking it, tilting my head… sometimes asking someone. It just kind of eats up time before I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face is something you do to try to make your anxiety about it go away?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s what I’m trying to do. Like if I check my face in the camera before sending event pictures or joining a vendor meeting, it feels like maybe I’ll calm down. But then I end up retaking photos or tilting my head, or asking a coworker if I look weird, and it can take long enough that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: Not really a number, no. It’s more like… before I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I keep checking my face in the phone camera, retaking it, tilting my head a certain way. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird. I stop when it feels “okay” enough, I guess, but it can take a while.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face, are you trying to make sure you look perfect or are you trying to avoid looking embarrassing to others?\n\nPatient: More avoiding embarrassing myself, I think. Like before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I keep checking my face in my phone camera, retaking photos, tilting my head… sometimes asking a coworker if I look weird. It can take long enough that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face, is it because you're afraid of what others will think of you, or is it solely because you don't like how you look yourself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more because I’m scared other people will think I look weird. Like before vendor meetings or sending event pictures, I’ll check my camera and retake things or angle my head… sometimes ask a coworker. It just takes longer than it should.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s about my face. Since I’ve had to be in front of people more at work, I keep noticing this one part that looks uneven in photos. Other people say they can barely see it, but I can’t really stop looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Is it always this one part of your face that bothers you, or do you notice other areas as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly that one part of my face. It just looks uneven to me, especially in photos. I know other people say they barely notice anything, but… I keep looking at it anyway.\n\nDoctor: How much distress does thinking about this uneven part of your face cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty upsetting. I mean, that’s kind of why I came in. Since I’ve been in front of people more at work, I keep staring at that uneven part in photos, even though people tell me they barely see it. I just can’t seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the uneven part of your face been affecting your work or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly at work. I missed part of a planning deadline because I got stuck worrying about how I looked in the event materials. And now even things like vendor calls or meeting prep take me longer than they should.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry more about the uneven part of your face itself, or about what others might think of it?\n\nPatient: More about the part itself, I think. Like… I keep seeing it in photos, especially now that I’m more visible at work. Other people say they barely notice anything, but I still can’t stop staring at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your life would be significantly better if you could just fix this uneven part of your face, regardless of what others think?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it would. That’s kind of why I came in. Since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, I just keep staring at that one uneven-looking part of my face in photos, even when everyone says they barely see it.\n\nDoctor: When you're worrying about the uneven part of your face, are you more concerned about the way it looks to you or the way you think it might look to others?\n\nPatient: I think… more the way it looks to me. I mean, I know other people say they barely see anything, but in photos I just keep staring at that one uneven part. It’s hard to look past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or actions in an attempt to reduce your anxiety about the uneven part of your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Like before I send event photos or get on a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in my phone camera a few times, retake pictures, tilt my head different ways… sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, but it can take long enough that I get behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors, like retaking pictures or asking coworkers, in order to feel okay about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. I don’t really feel settled unless I check first. Like before sending photos or getting on a vendor meeting, I’ll look in my phone camera, retake pictures, tilt my head… sometimes ask someone if I look weird. It can take up enough time that I fall behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're checking your face in the phone camera or asking coworkers about how you look?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I start out thinking I’ll just check once, but then I keep looking in the camera or retaking it, trying different angles. And sometimes I still end up asking a coworker… It can kind of take over and make me late on planning things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checking behaviors are something you can stop doing if you want to?\n\nPatient: I mean… I can stop eventually, but not really when I want to. If I’m about to send event pictures or get on a vendor call, I end up checking my face in the camera, retaking it, tilting my head… sometimes asking someone. It just kind of eats up time before I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face is something you do to try to make your anxiety about it go away?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s what I’m trying to do. Like if I check my face in the camera before sending event pictures or joining a vendor meeting, it feels like maybe I’ll calm down. But then I end up retaking photos or tilting my head, or asking a coworker if I look weird, and it can take long enough that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: Not really a number, no. It’s more like… before I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I keep checking my face in the phone camera, retaking it, tilting my head a certain way. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird. I stop when it feels “okay” enough, I guess, but it can take a while.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face, are you trying to make sure you look perfect or are you trying to avoid looking embarrassing to others?\n\nPatient: More avoiding embarrassing myself, I think. Like before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I keep checking my face in my phone camera, retaking photos, tilting my head… sometimes asking a coworker if I look weird. It can take long enough that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face, is it because you're afraid of what others will think of you, or is it solely because you don't like how you look yourself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more because I’m scared other people will think I look weird. Like before vendor meetings or sending event pictures, I’ll check my camera and retake things or angle my head… sometimes ask a coworker. It just takes longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face, is it more about fixing the uneven part you see or avoiding what you think others might see?\n\nPatient: I guess it starts with that uneven part I see in my face, especially in photos. But it’s mostly because I’m worried other people will notice it too, even though they usually say they barely see anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like before I send event photos or get on a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in my phone camera a few times, retake pictures, tilt my head different ways… sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, but it can take long enough that I get behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors, like retaking pictures or asking coworkers, in order to feel okay about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. I don’t really feel settled unless I check first. Like before sending photos or getting on a vendor meeting, I’ll look in my phone camera, retake pictures, tilt my head… sometimes ask someone if I look weird. It can take up enough time that I fall behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're checking your face in the phone camera or asking coworkers about how you look?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I start out thinking I’ll just check once, but then I keep looking in the camera or retaking it, trying different angles. And sometimes I still end up asking a coworker… It can kind of take over and make me late on planning things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checking behaviors are something you can stop doing if you want to?\n\nPatient: I mean… I can stop eventually, but not really when I want to. If I’m about to send event pictures or get on a vendor call, I end up checking my face in the camera, retaking it, tilting my head… sometimes asking someone. It just kind of eats up time before I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face is something you do to try to make your anxiety about it go away?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s what I’m trying to do. Like if I check my face in the camera before sending event pictures or joining a vendor meeting, it feels like maybe I’ll calm down. But then I end up retaking photos or tilting my head, or asking a coworker if I look weird, and it can take long enough that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: Not really a number, no. It’s more like… before I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I keep checking my face in the phone camera, retaking it, tilting my head a certain way. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird. I stop when it feels “okay” enough, I guess, but it can take a while.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face, are you trying to make sure you look perfect or are you trying to avoid looking embarrassing to others?\n\nPatient: More avoiding embarrassing myself, I think. Like before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I keep checking my face in my phone camera, retaking photos, tilting my head… sometimes asking a coworker if I look weird. It can take long enough that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face, is it because you're afraid of what others will think of you, or is it solely because you don't like how you look yourself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more because I’m scared other people will think I look weird. Like before vendor meetings or sending event pictures, I’ll check my camera and retake things or angle my head… sometimes ask a coworker. It just takes longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face, is it more about fixing the uneven part you see or avoiding what you think others might see?\n\nPatient: I guess it starts with that uneven part I see in my face, especially in photos. But it’s mostly because I’m worried other people will notice it too, even though they usually say they barely see anything.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face, is it because you're trying to fix what you see as uneven or because you're afraid of being judged by others?\n\nPatient: Both, kind of… I’m trying to fix how uneven it looks, like with angles or retaking pictures. But mostly it’s because I don’t want people in a vendor meeting or seeing event photos to think I look weird. I’ll check my phone camera or ask a coworker, and then I end up wasting time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s about my face. Since I’ve had to be in front of people more at work, I keep noticing this one part that looks uneven in photos. Other people say they can barely see it, but I can’t really stop looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Is it always this one part of your face that bothers you, or do you notice other areas as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly that one part of my face. It just looks uneven to me, especially in photos. I know other people say they barely notice anything, but… I keep looking at it anyway.\n\nDoctor: How much distress does thinking about this uneven part of your face cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s… pretty upsetting. I mean, that’s kind of why I came in. Since I’ve been in front of people more at work, I keep staring at that uneven part in photos, even though people tell me they barely see it. I just can’t seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the uneven part of your face been affecting your work or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly at work. I missed part of a planning deadline because I got stuck worrying about how I looked in the event materials. And now even things like vendor calls or meeting prep take me longer than they should.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry more about the uneven part of your face itself, or about what others might think of it?\n\nPatient: More about the part itself, I think. Like… I keep seeing it in photos, especially now that I’m more visible at work. Other people say they barely notice anything, but I still can’t stop staring at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your life would be significantly better if you could just fix this uneven part of your face, regardless of what others think?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it would. That’s kind of why I came in. Since I’ve had to be more in front of people at work, I just keep staring at that one uneven-looking part of my face in photos, even when everyone says they barely see it.\n\nDoctor: When you're worrying about the uneven part of your face, are you more concerned about the way it looks to you or the way you think it might look to others?\n\nPatient: I think… more the way it looks to me. I mean, I know other people say they barely see anything, but in photos I just keep staring at that one uneven part. It’s hard to look past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or actions in an attempt to reduce your anxiety about the uneven part of your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Like before I send event photos or get on a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face in my phone camera a few times, retake pictures, tilt my head different ways… sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, but it can take long enough that I get behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors, like retaking pictures or asking coworkers, in order to feel okay about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… kind of. I don’t really feel settled unless I check first. Like before sending photos or getting on a vendor meeting, I’ll look in my phone camera, retake pictures, tilt my head… sometimes ask someone if I look weird. It can take up enough time that I fall behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're checking your face in the phone camera or asking coworkers about how you look?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I start out thinking I’ll just check once, but then I keep looking in the camera or retaking it, trying different angles. And sometimes I still end up asking a coworker… It can kind of take over and make me late on planning things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checking behaviors are something you can stop doing if you want to?\n\nPatient: I mean… I can stop eventually, but not really when I want to. If I’m about to send event pictures or get on a vendor call, I end up checking my face in the camera, retaking it, tilting my head… sometimes asking someone. It just kind of eats up time before I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face is something you do to try to make your anxiety about it go away?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s what I’m trying to do. Like if I check my face in the camera before sending event pictures or joining a vendor meeting, it feels like maybe I’ll calm down. But then I end up retaking photos or tilting my head, or asking a coworker if I look weird, and it can take long enough that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: Not really a number, no. It’s more like… before I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I keep checking my face in the phone camera, retaking it, tilting my head a certain way. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird. I stop when it feels “okay” enough, I guess, but it can take a while.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face, are you trying to make sure you look perfect or are you trying to avoid looking embarrassing to others?\n\nPatient: More avoiding embarrassing myself, I think. Like before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I keep checking my face in my phone camera, retaking photos, tilting my head… sometimes asking a coworker if I look weird. It can take long enough that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face, is it because you're afraid of what others will think of you, or is it solely because you don't like how you look yourself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more because I’m scared other people will think I look weird. Like before vendor meetings or sending event pictures, I’ll check my camera and retake things or angle my head… sometimes ask a coworker. It just takes longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face, is it more about fixing the uneven part you see or avoiding what you think others might see?\n\nPatient: I guess it starts with that uneven part I see in my face, especially in photos. But it’s mostly because I’m worried other people will notice it too, even though they usually say they barely see anything.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face, is it because you're trying to fix what you see as uneven or because you're afraid of being judged by others?\n\nPatient: Both, kind of… I’m trying to fix how uneven it looks, like with angles or retaking pictures. But mostly it’s because I don’t want people in a vendor meeting or seeing event photos to think I look weird. I’ll check my phone camera or ask a coworker, and then I end up wasting time.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face, is the main reason you're doing it because you genuinely believe it looks uneven or because you're afraid of what others might think if they see it?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s because I really do see it as uneven. Like, I get stuck staring at that one part of my face in photos, especially now that I’m in front of people more at work. Other people say they barely notice anything, but I still can’t really let it go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It just kind of eats up time before I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your face is something you do to try to make your anxiety about it go away?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s what I’m trying to do. Like if I check my face in the camera before sending event pictures or joining a vendor meeting, it feels like maybe I’ll calm down. But then I end up retaking photos or tilting my head, or asking a coworker if I look weird, and it can take long enough that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face a certain number of times before you can stop?\n\nPatient: Not really a number, no. It’s more like… before I send event pictures or get on a vendor meeting, I keep checking my face in the phone camera, retaking it, tilting my head a certain way. Sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look weird. I stop when it feels “okay” enough, I guess, but it can take a while.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face, are you trying to make sure you look perfect or are you trying to avoid looking embarrassing to others?\n\nPatient: More avoiding embarrassing myself, I think. Like before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I keep checking my face in my phone camera, retaking photos, tilting my head… sometimes asking a coworker if I look weird. It can take long enough that I fall behind on planning stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face, is it because you're afraid of what others will think of you, or is it solely because you don't like how you look yourself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more because I’m scared other people will think I look weird. Like before vendor meetings or sending event pictures, I’ll check my camera and retake things or angle my head… sometimes ask a coworker. It just takes longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face, is it more about fixing the uneven part you see or avoiding what you think others might see?\n\nPatient: I guess it starts with that uneven part I see in my face, especially in photos. But it’s mostly because I’m worried other people will notice it too, even though they usually say they barely see anything.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face, is it because you're trying to fix what you see as uneven or because you're afraid of being judged by others?\n\nPatient: Both, kind of… I’m trying to fix how uneven it looks, like with angles or retaking pictures. But mostly it’s because I don’t want people in a vendor meeting or seeing event photos to think I look weird. I’ll check my phone camera or ask a coworker, and then I end up wasting time.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face, is the main reason you're doing it because you genuinely believe it looks uneven or because you're afraid of what others might think if they see it?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s because I really do see it as uneven. Like, I get stuck staring at that one part of my face in photos, especially now that I’m in front of people more at work. Other people say they barely notice anything, but I still can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face, is it more about making sure you look acceptable to yourself or making sure you look acceptable to others?\n\nPatient: Probably more to others… like before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face on my phone, retake stuff, tilt my head, sometimes ask a coworker if I look weird. I don’t like admitting it, but it can take long enough that I get behind on planning things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Like before vendor meetings or sending event pictures, I’ll check my camera and retake things or angle my head… sometimes ask a coworker. It just takes longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face, is it more about fixing the uneven part you see or avoiding what you think others might see?\n\nPatient: I guess it starts with that uneven part I see in my face, especially in photos. But it’s mostly because I’m worried other people will notice it too, even though they usually say they barely see anything.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face, is it because you're trying to fix what you see as uneven or because you're afraid of being judged by others?\n\nPatient: Both, kind of… I’m trying to fix how uneven it looks, like with angles or retaking pictures. But mostly it’s because I don’t want people in a vendor meeting or seeing event photos to think I look weird. I’ll check my phone camera or ask a coworker, and then I end up wasting time.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face, is the main reason you're doing it because you genuinely believe it looks uneven or because you're afraid of what others might think if they see it?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s because I really do see it as uneven. Like, I get stuck staring at that one part of my face in photos, especially now that I’m in front of people more at work. Other people say they barely notice anything, but I still can’t really let it go.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking your face, is it more about making sure you look acceptable to yourself or making sure you look acceptable to others?\n\nPatient: Probably more to others… like before I send event pictures or join a vendor meeting, I’ll check my face on my phone, retake stuff, tilt my head, sometimes ask a coworker if I look weird. I don’t like admitting it, but it can take long enough that I get behind on planning things.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern that your face looks uneven to you, or that others will judge you because of it?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s more that it looks uneven to me. Like, I keep noticing this one part of my face in photos, especially now that I’m in front of people more for work. Other people say they barely see it, but I still get stuck on it."
